주가 추이

3Q 큰 폭의 판매 감소, 매출/영업이익 시장 기대치 하회 예상 
3Q26 동사 매출액과 영 업 이 익 은 각각 44.6조원(-4.5% 이하 Y oY)과 2.4조원
(-5.4%) 기록하며 시장 기대치 하회 전망. 3Q 매출액은 글로벌 판매 부진 지속
과 환율 하락, 그리고 친환경차 판매 성장 둔화 등의 영향에 기인. 특히, 3Q 판
매량은 918.7만대(-11.5% 이하 Y oY)로 전년 동기 대비 큰 폭 감소하며 매출
액에 부정적 영향. 이는 7월과 8월 발생한 파업 영향으로 약 5.5만대 규모의 생
산 물량 차질이 발 생 한 부분에 기인. 파업은 기존 물량/믹스 개선에 기 여 했 던 
글로벌 HEV 판매도 둔화(+3.4%)시키면서 매출에 부정적 영향을 심화. 
3Q 수 익 성 은 4~5월 상승했던 원자재 투입에 따른 원가 상승 영향과 친환경차 
판매 감소에 따른 부정적 믹스 영향 불 가 피. 이에 시장 기대치인 3.0조원을 크
게 하회할 전망. 다만, 기말 환율이 1,356원/$ 수준으로 낮아지면서 판매보증비 
외화환산손익 영향은 다소 개선(0.4%p) 예상되며, 관세 영향 또한 전년 동기 
대비 약 1조원(2.1%p) 감소하면서 수익성 악화 영향을 일부 만 회 할 전망.  
’27년 지연된 신차효과 가시화로 반등 여력 충분 
4Q부터는 3Q 파업 영향 등으로 지연된 신차 확대 본격화되고, 현지 수요 높은 
차종 중심의 물량 확대를 통해 매출 회복 및 이익 성장 가시화가 기대. 아 반 떼
와 투싼 FMC 확대로 국내 및 해외 판매 둔화가 점진적 만회되고, 지난 9월부
터 터키에서 아이오닉3 생산이 본격화되면서 유럽에서의 시장 부진 회복이 기
대. 또한, GV80과 G80 HEV 모델 출시로 내년부터 국내/북미 중심 판매 확대
되며 올해 부진했던 물량/믹스 개선에 기여할 것으로 기대. ’27년 HMGMA에
서 HEV 신차 생산 투입 예정되어 있어 상 대 적 으 로 높은 수요가 지속되는 북미
에서의 HEV 현지 생산 확대로 가동률 증 가 와 관세 부담 완화도 수익성 개선에 
기여할 것으로 기대. 
부정적 환율 고려 필요하나 ’27년 강한 증익 기대  
’27년 동사 영 업 이 익 추 정 치 는 12.4조원(+22.3%)으로 ’26년 감 익 에 서 ’27년 
증익 전환 예상. 최근 환율 추세 고려 시 내년 상 반 기 까 지 부정적인 환율 영향 
확대 가 능 성 존재하나, 기존 노후 차 종 에 서 현지 수요 대응 가능한 신차 중심 
생산/판매 확대로 물량/믹스 개선되며 부 정 적 환율 영향 상쇄 기대.  
12M EPS 추정치 하향(-11.8%)과 상반기 적 용 한 코 스 피 프리미엄 제거(-
10%)에 따라 목 표 주 가 를 670,000원으로 하향. 그러나, 연 내 RMAC 운영 
성 과 와 RA 설립, 12 월 모셔널 자 율 주 행 상용화와 웨 이 모向 로 보 택 시 
양산/공급 등 미래 사 업 에 서 의 가 시 성 도 여전히 기대 가능한 부분.

이 자료는 조사분석 담당자가 객관적 사실에 근거해 작성하였으며, 타인의 부당한 압력이나 간섭없이 본인의 견을 정확하게 
반영했습니다. 본인은 이 자료에서 다룬 종목과 관련해 공표일 현재 관련 법규상 알려야 할 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다. 본인은 이 
자료를 기관투자자 또는 제 3 자에게 사전에 제공한 사실이 없습니다. (김성래) 
저희 회사는 공표일 현재 이 자료에서 다룬 종목의 발행주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다. 
이 자료는 투자자의 증권투자를 돕기 위해 당사 고객에 한하여 배포되는 자료로서 저작권이 당사에 있으며 불법 복제 및 배포를 금
합니다. 이 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료나 정보출처로부터 얻은 것이지만, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완
전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 이 자료는 어떠한 경우에도 고객의 증권투자 결과와 관련된 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙으로 사
용될 수 없습니다.

### Page 8

현대차 (005380) [한화리서치]

8 
[ 종목 투자등급 ] 
당사는 개별 종목에 대해 향후 1 년간 +15% 이상의 절대수익률이 기대되는 종목에 대해 Buy(매수) 의견을 제시합니다. 또한 절대수
익률 -15~+15%가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Hold(보유) 의견을, -15% 이하가 예상되는 종목에 대해 Sell(매도) 의견을 제시합니다. 밸류
에이션 방법 등 절대수익률 산정은 개별 종목을 커버하는 애널리스트의 추정에 따르며, 목표주가 산정이나 투자의견 변경 주기는 종
목별로 다릅니다.

[ 산업 투자의견 ] 
당사는 산업에 대해 향후 1 년간 해당 업종의 수익률이 과거 수익률에 비해 양호한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Positive(긍정
적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 또한 향후 1 년간 수익률이 과거 수익률과 유사한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Neutral(중립적) 
의견을, 과거 수익률보다 부진한 흐름을 보일 것으로 예상되는 경우에 Negative(부정적) 의견을 제시하고 있습니다. 산업별 수익률 전
망은 해당 산업 내 분석대상 종목들에 대한 담당 애널리스트의 분석과 판단에 따릅니다.

[ 당사 조사분석자료의 투자등급 부여 비중 ] (기준일: 2026 년 9 월 30 일) 
투자등급 매수 중립 매도 합계 
금융투자상품의 비중 96.4% 3.6% 0.0% 100.0%

## Document 167: Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0

Source PDF: all report pdf/Allianz_2026-09-17_Chokepoints and pressure points what is breaking and reshaping global energy markets_445c9ff0.pdf
Report source: Allianz Trade
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1

In Summary 
• The ongoing oil-price shock shouldn’t prove structural, with flows likely to normalize in 
2027. But several pressure points are pulling oil and gas prices in different directions 
and the balance will decide where prices go next.  Brent jumped to nearly 110 USD/bbl 
after Saudi Arabia was forced to shut its East-West pipeline, pushing the net hit to Hormuz 
flows to about 4.3mbpd vs pre -war levels.  For now, pressure points (including strategic 
reserves near historic lows  and an underinvested refining base) are outweighing the two 
relief valves (shadow traffic in Hormuz and demand destruction). As we expect the Saudi 
pipeline to be repaired rather swiftly and volumes steady in Hormuz,  Brent should average 
87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before consolidating to 76 USD/bbl in 2027. But prices would hold 
near 100 USD/bbl if the outage extends toward six weeks and Hormuz remains in flux.  
• European gas prices are climbing as the region races to refill, though the resulting 
macro risk looks contained for now. Gas prices have rallied from the mid-40 EUR/MWh in 
July to over 80 EUR/MWh as Hormuz LNG loadings stay stuck at 15-25% of pre-war levels. 
We expect TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh in Q4 2026 before easing to 36 EUR/MWh in 2027 
amid larger supply from both the US and the Middle  East. A sustained move above 120  
EUR/MWh for six months would risk tipping the Eurozone into recession.  
• A separate refining and export bottleneck is keeping refined product prices elevated. 
US and European crack spreads have surged to 62 EUR/bbl and 74 EUR/bbl. On top of 
Hormuz disruptions, a significant share of Russia’s refining capacity has been  hit by 
Ukrainian strikes, resulting in  a diesel export ban. Until June, China also had export 
restrictions in place amid fear s of supply shortage s. A structurally underinvested refining 
base is adding to the multi -layered crisis . As a result,  global oil demand is expected to 
contract 2.5mbpd in 2026 as demand from Asia has collapsed. 
• Diesel-reliant transport and agriculture, Asian petrochemicals and fertilizers are the 
most exposed sectors  with very few short -term substitutes available , while producers 
with domestic feedstock flexibility are comparatively insulated.  Between March and 
August, fossil -fuel importers paid an estimated extra USD330bn. High d iesel prices are 
hitting trucking, agriculture and construction, and the Gulf’s 40% share of global sulfur 
exports is impacting food production via fertilizer s. The n ear-term adjustment is mainly 
through inventory drawdowns and output cuts rather than genuine substitution.  
• Households are only starting to feel the full inflationary hit, and the pass -through will 
remain sticky into 2027.  In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of August’s CPI 
increase, with pump prices set to reach 3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y, about 355 USD 
more per household annually). In Europe, regulated tariffs are catching up with a lag and 
lower-income households remain disproportionately exposed.  
• Bonds are already pricing  in most of the  energy premium, but sustained inflation and 
oil-price spikes could lead to further rate stress. Much of the initial repricing has already 
occurred. We estimate that a further increase in Brent to 120 USD/bbl would only lift yields 
by 5-10bps (vs 17-18bps on inflation swaps and 8-11bps on expected policy-rate pricing). 
This suggests that a significant further rise in yields would likely require a renewed large 
supply shock or evidence that inflation is delaying monetary -policy normalization. 
• Looking ahead, over the longer run, the unfolding events  are fundamentally a 
Hormuz/Gulf shock  that will reshape global energy  markets. The conflict is reshaping 
global energy trade routes: Gulf states are building out bypass infrastructure (ADCOP, the 
Kirkuk-Ceyhan/Development Road corridor, Duqm) while China, India, Japan and South 
Korea diversify away from Gulf -transiting barrels; LNG has no equivalent bypass, so gas 
exposure remains structural.

Chokepoints and pressure points : what is breaking 
and reshaping global energy markets

### Page 2

Roller-coaster oil markets  
The recent attack on Saudi Arabia’s East -West pipeline (Petroline), combined with stalled talks between Iran 
and Gulf states on Hormuz shipping, is compounding risks, but we expect the shock to fade. Brent has spiked to 
nearly 110  USD/bbl, widening an already -significant Persian Gulf supply gap (see Figure 1): despite lower Gulf 
production, exports proved more resilient, but the net hit to Hormuz flows still stands at about 4.3mbpd versus pre -
war levels. It remains uncertain when the S audi pipeline will resume normal flows of 7mbpd - a mild scenario sees 
volumes ramp back to 5-6mbpd within weeks, while a worst case keeps the pipeline shut for about six week but we 
see this as a temporary shock either way.

OECD commercial stocks remain the main shock absorber, but that buffer is thinning fast. US Strategic Petroleum 
Reserve draws have slowed to just 0.3mbpd over the past month - reserves are now near their lowest level in 
decades, so Washington has little scope to repeat the scale of releases seen in 2022  or earlier this year even if the 
deficit widens further ; while Japan and South Korea are rebuilding their own reserves rather than drawing them 
down. China’s crude imports, meanwhile, are only just restarting after months of weakness and remain about 
4mbpd below year-ago levels; refiners are taking advantage of firm margins and spare quota to buy and reprocess 
crude for re-export rather than to meet stronger domestic demand, so this is not yet a genuine demand recovery. 
Against this backdrop, we forecast Brent averaging 88 USD/bbl in Q3 and 87 USD/bbl in Q4 2026 before easing to 
76 USD/bbl in 2027 - but a deeper or more prolonged Saudi export disruption, without an offsetting reserve release, 
would keep the market in deficit for longer and raise the probability of Brent holding near 100  USD/bbl.

A natural gas squeeze looming over Europe? 
Natural gas prices are ticking up as Europe races to refill storage.  European gas is exposed to the same Middle 
East disruption through a different channel: LNG loadings through Hormuz have remained stuck at 15 -25% of pre-
war levels for two months, even as Qatar sends more empty tankers into the Gulf. We expect Qatari and Emirati 
LNG exports to normalize only in Q1 2027, so for TTF to keep pricing enough Asian LNG demand out of the market 
to free cargoes for Europe, prices must stay elevated for longer: the rally from the mid -40 EUR/MWh in July to over 
80 EUR/MWh has already cut some demand from the rest of the world. With Europe still playing catch -up on 
storage (see Figure 2), we expect TTF to average 65 EUR/MWh in both Q3 and Q4 2026 before consolidating to 36 
EUR/MWh in 2027 as Hormuz flows normalize and incremental US LNG supply arrives. The key near -term risk is 
storage: a cold winter combined with a lower-than-usual fill could deplete storage before spring, front-loading price 
pressure into early 2027.

### Page 4

For now, the macro risk from gas prices looks contained.  In 2022, gas prices averaged close to 130 EUR/MWh for 
a full year, yet the Eurozone still grew about 3.4%, cushioned by a post -pandemic demand boom and a fiscal 
response worth an estimated 0.4pp of growth. Today’s backdrop is less forgiving: fiscal space has narrowed and 
household demand is far less dynamic. We think TTF in the 80 -100 EUR/MWh range could be absorbed for nine 
months or more without a growth shock, but a sustained move above 120 EUR/MWh for six months or longer would 
tip the Eurozone into recession. Our base case, TTF averaging 65 EUR/MWh through Q3 and Q4 2026, does not test 
that threshold, though a winter upside scenario would keep the tail risk alive.  
Refined products to remain expensive  
A two -layer infrastructure bottleneck is hitting refined products and will keep driving regional prices up, 
independent of crude.  In August, Brent averaged 89  USD/bbl, yet clean -product crack spreads surged to record 
levels, the US benchmark reached 62  EUR/bbl and European diesel crack 74 EUR/bbl (68% and 92% above their 
five-year averages) showing the main story is refining availability, not crude input costs. Three regions are driving 
the crunch. In the Middle East, the US -Iran conflict and Hormuz blockade have hit both refinery operations and 
product exports: pre -conflict flows of roughly  6mbpd through the Strait have fallen to 1.1mbpd, and the Saudi 
pipeline attack compounds this by also halting flows to the Yanbu refineries. In Russia, Ukrainian strikes cut refinery 
runs to multi -year lows by mid -May, and Moscow’s diesel export ban - extended through September, with no 
guarantee against further extension - has squeezed a market that depends on Russia for 11% of global diesel. In 
China, export restrictions imposed in March to preserve domestic inventories cut clean -product exports by 24.3% 
year-on-year over the first five months, though Beijing’s June release of 13mn tons should ease global supply, lifting 
China’s exports full-year total to 32mn tons, 0.6% above last year. 
Figure 3: US and Europe crack spread historical prices (USD/bbl)

Sources: 
 LSEG Workspace
 , Allianz Research

Global clean-product inventories are drying up as the crisis extends through 2026.  European (ARA) inventories 
are well below the five -year average. In the US, refiners are running at record utilization (98% in September), but 
most of the extra output is being exported for higher margins rather than sold domestically — gasoline stocks are 
6% and diesel 14% below seasonal norms, and US diesel topped 6  USD/gallon for the first time. Rising domestic 
prices could eventually pull barrels back into the US market and curb these exports, with some early – tough strongly 
pushed back by government officials for now  - talks about potential diesel export restrictions to support domestic 
supply. Additionally, there is little relief from spare capacity: years of underinvestment and refinery closures have 
left the West structurally unprepared, global refining investment is on track to fall below 40  USD bn this year (-15% 
y/y), and capacity is set to grow only marginally through 2040. Unlike 2022, when Europe and Asia reallocated 
capital toward LNG regasification within months, refining capacity cannot be added quickly enough to ease this 
shortage in the short or medium term, and rebuilding inventories will take time even once Hormuz flows normalize. 
We now expect world oil demand to contract by 2.5mbpd in 2026 as high product prices force further demand 
destruction, concentrated in middle distillates and petrochemical feedstocks in Asia. Longer term, persistently 
expensive products should accelerate substitution toward EVs and heat pumps, and put a premium on refineries 
with the flexibility to shift crude grades and product mix.

Downstream sector impact and potential substitution 
After six months, duration, and not just the peak price, is now the central macro risk. Between March and August, 
fossil-fuel importers paid an estimated 330  USD bn more than pre-war forward prices implied, split roughly evenly 
between crude and refined products - a real income transfer from importers to exporters that squeezes household 
purchasing power and corporate margins, weakens currencies, and strains public finances wherever governments 
subsidize fuel. The Hormuz crisis is unusual in disrupting many linked -but-hard-to-substitute commodities at once 
(crude, gas, LPG, naphtha and sulfur ) spilling into fertilizer and petrochemical shortages. Substitution can happen 
at three levels : (i) switching energy carriers, (ii) switching industrial feedstocks, or (iii) consumers switching final 
products. With the first working fastest but simply redistributing scarcity, and the other two developing more slowly 
but reshaping industrial structure permanently. Inflation itself spreads in three stages: a fast first -round hit to 
energy-intensive industries (speed depending on taxes and subsidies), a slower production -cost effect as old-price 
inventories and contracts run out, and a second -round effect as wages, inflation expectations and price -setting 
behavior adjust. 
The immediate impact is on energy -intensive industries.  Gulf states are major exporters of both fuels and 
feedstocks: Europe is less directly exposed to Gulf crude than East Asia but still feels it through global oil prices, LNG 
competition and product shortages; the US is more insulated on crude but exposed via global product prices, hitting 
lower-income consumers hardest while producers and refiners gain from higher margins. East and South Asia face 
the largest physical exposure, with China, India and Japan recording the highest net additional fossil -fuel import 
costs since the conflict began – USD 31.7 bn, USD 14.4bn and USD 10.4bn respectively. Diesel is the most powerful 
transmission channel given its role in trucking, agriculture, mining and construction - sectors that are hard to 
electrify, especially for long -haul transport and in emerging markets - while shipping and aviation face higher 
bunker/jet-fuel costs and rerouting. Among industrial inputs, East Asian petrochemical crackers reliant on imported 
naphtha are most exposed, whereas the US benefits from domestic ethane and China retains partial cover via coal-
based chemicals; metals, cement and glass are exposed through electricity and high-temperature heat costs, hitting 
aluminum hardest. Gulf countries supply more than 40% of global sulfur exports and about 20% of 
ammonia/nitrogen-fertilizer exports, so the crisis strikes food production through fertilizer, diesel, irrigation and 
packaging costs simultaneously. 
The second-round effect on the broader economy will be felt with a lag and last longer.  Food shows this best: 
packaged food embeds fertilizer, farm fuel, irrigation, refrigeration, processing heat, packaging, transport and 
labor, each repricing at a different speed : fresh produce reacts within weeks, grain can take up to a year via fertilizer 
and harvest effects, and restaurant prices adjust slowest but most persistently as labor and rent costs catch up. 
Large retailers can hedge and absorb costs better than small one s, and real household income falls as consumers 
trade down and defer spending. Export restrictions, subsidies and price controls can temporarily shield domestic 
food prices but tend to redistribute scarcity and worsen the burden on lower -income, food-importing economies.

### Page 6

6

Construction and real estate are hit both directly (cement, plastics, metals, diesel) and, more durably, indirectly 
through higher interest rates raising financing costs and lowering the present value of future rents. Compared with 
2022, the economy enters this shock with more renewable capacity and European LNG infrastructure - renewables 
installed since 2020 reportedly avoided USD 36bn of fossil -fuel imports in the first five months of the crisis - but 
public debt is higher and buffers are thinner after the pandemic and the 2022 crisis.  
Substitution will spread across fuels, feedstocks and products but initially redistributes inflation rather than 
eliminating it. The first response is inventory drawdowns, switching to costlier available inputs, output cuts and cost 
pass-through. Over time it becomes more structural, reorganizing downstream markets around flexibility, lower 
energy intensity and redundancy - persistently expensive oil and gas improve the economics of electrification, even 
though electrification changes commodity dependence rather than removing it. Because capital, regulatory 
approval and margins all constrain the pace of change, expect a two -stage adjustment: curtailment and demand 
destruction first, structural substitution and capex later , alongside rising demand for fuel/feedstock flexibility even 
where it costs more than a specialized, single-fuel plant.

Households to face higher inflation 
Six months in, households are losing purchasing power in stages.  Petrol and diesel reprice within days or weeks, 
but gas and electricity bills follow regulated tariff calendars and contract -renewal dates, so the current inflation 
reading is not yet the peak. Exposure varies sharply by household - an EV- and heat-pump-owning homeowner is 
comparatively insulated, a gas-heated renter won’t feel the full effect until tariffs reset, and a rural household reliant 
on diesel cars feels it immediately. Petrol and diesel are the fastest channel: European pu mp prices typically pass 
through crude costs within one to two months, roughly one -for-one. In the US, gasoline alone drove over a third of 
August’s CPI increase, diesel peaked above 6  USD/gallon in September, and average pump prices are on track for 
3.84 USD/gallon in 2026 (+24% y/y) - about USD 355 more per year for a typical household; hitting rural, low-income 
households hardest given limited access to public transport.  
Gas and electricity bills rise on institutional timetables, so the pass -through is felt with a lag that varies by 
market. In France, the regulated gas reference price rises 6.3% on October 1 (to 182.88  EUR/MWh including tax, 
about 60 EUR/year for indexed contracts), though roughly 40% of households are on fixed-price contracts and won’t 
feel it until renewal; French electricity moves more slowly, with the next adjustment scheduled for February 2027. 
The UK’s quarterly price cap rises by a bout 5  GBP/month from October, partly offset by government VAT relief, 
while roughly 35% of households on fixed contracts are shielded for now. Japan and India rely more on subsidy 
calendars than market pass -through, which smooths the household shock across time wit hout removing its fiscal 
cost. More broadly, real energy bills were still about 4% above 2019 levels in 2024, so this shock lands on budgets 
that had not fully recovered from 2022 ; and unevenly: an estimated 120mn households in advanced economies 
spend over 10% of income on home energy, rising to 20% including transport for the poorest, and the bottom income 
quintile spent 12% of disposable income on gas, electricity and heating in 2022 versus 4% for the top quintile.

In the longer term, the energy crisis impacts inflation beyond energy bills.  A second inflationary wave builds as 
transport and industrial costs feed into food, goods and services with a lag of around two years: a permanent 10% 
rise in oil prices raises headline CPI by about 2.3%, food CPI by 0.3% and core CPI by 0.1%, and after e xpenditure 
weights, headline CPI rises by almost 0.4% - consistent with the roughly 0.5% inflation contribution the last oil move 
added in the Eurozone, UK and Canada. Looking ahead, households are likely to weigh energy resilience more 
heavily in purchasing decisions, shifting spending from variable fuel cost s toward upfront capex on electrification 
and insulation - even as higher prices simultaneously squeeze the ability to finance that switch, widening inequality 
in energy exposure. Electricity is set to become the central household energy price, trading oil and gas exposure for 
exposure to power prices, network ch arges and time -of-use pricing, with government subsidies and price -cap 
calendars still deciding when the crisis is actually felt.

The energy-yield loop 
Energy shocks often have an immediate effect on rates through several components.  The first one is inflation 
expectations: Higher commodity prices tend to increase inflation and are priced directly on the derivatives market . 
Historically a 1% rise in Brent lifts the 2Y inflation swap by 1. 65bps in the US and 1. 77bps in the Eurozone, and the 
1y1y forward inflation swap by 0.64bp in the US and 1.09bp in the Eurozone. During special shocks the effect 
intensifies: The US 2Y inflation swap moved 1. 86bps per 1% of oil during the Ukraine shock and 2. 01bps during the 
Iran episode. The EU 2Y inflation swap moved by 2.60bps during the Ukraine shock, but only 0.36bp during the Iran 
episode. 
The first channel then impacts  the expected short rates path.  When inflation tends to increase suddenly, the 
central bank faces more pressure  to be hawkish because of market pricing . Historically a 1% rise in Brent adds 
1.02bp to US monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y OIS − Fed Funds) and 0.83bp to EUR monetary pricing (proxied by 2Y 
OIS − €STR). During crises , this pressure concentrates region by region: US pricing repriced hardest during the 
Ukraine shock at 2.82bps per 1% of oil, close to three times its long -run sensitivity, while in the current Iran episode

it is EUR pricing that dominates at 2.40bps. This second channel tends to move short rates (more sensitive to inflation 
and monetary policy) (<2Y maturities: US 2Y +0.95bp, DE 2Y +0.53bp per 1% of oil) and the "belly" (more sensitive 
to growth and the economy) (5Y –7Y: US 5Y +0.98bp, DE 5Y +0.54bp) fa ster than long rates (which embed more 
fundamental factors such as demography, fiscal situation, political stability) (>10Y maturity: US 30Y +0.65bp, DE 
30Y +0.50bp). When the front end increases more than the long end, it induces a bear flattening , which hurts the 
economy more broadly. This tends to tighten financial conditions by increasing borrowing costs, slowing the growth 
of production and credit, but also by increasing transport costs, making it harder for commodities trading houses to 
provide more inputs to markets due to their short -term financing at 3M.

In times of shocks, the sensitivities of commodities on rates roughly double . On the German curve, a 1% rise in 
Brent moves the 2Y yield by 0.53bp historically versus 1.43bp in the current episode, and EUR monetary pricing by 
0.83bp versus 2.40bp. Per +10% move in Brent, that is about +5bps on the German curve and +9bps on the US curve 
in normal times, rising to about +14bps on the German 2Y and +12bps on the US 10Y in shock, with the 2Y inflation 
swaps moving some + 17bps historically and up to + 20bps ina  shock.  Figure 9 illustrates the incremental impact 
on rates and inflation markets implied by a move in Brent to USD120, USD100 or USD80 from current levels. 
These scenario moves are separate from the repricing already observed during the current episode, much of 
which is already embedded in inflation compensation and monetary-policy expectations.

Bond markets have already absorbed a large portion of the current energy shock through higher inflation 
compensation and a repricing of the expected monetary -policy path. While additional oil-price gains would still 
exert upward pressure on rates, the scenario analysis suggests that the marginal impact is now smaller than during 
the initial phase of the shock. As a result, a sustained and significant further rise in yields would likely require a new 
escalation in supply disruptions or clearer evidence that energy -driven inflation is feeding into broader price 
dynamics and delaying the normalization of monetary policy.  
How the conflict is reshaping the global energy map 
The Strait of Hormuz has always been the Gulf’s structural vulnerability, and this crisis is accelerating a shift 
from managing chokepoint risk to actively engineering around it.  The attack on Saudi Arabia’s own hedge (i.e.  
Petroline) is a reminder that existing bypass infrastructure is now a target, not a guarantee. Riyadh’s repair timeline 
will determine how quickly that redundancy returns, but the episode has already revived talk of expanding capacity 
rather than just repairing it. The UAE’s ADCOP pipeline to Fujairah, which sits outside the Strait entirely, remains the 
cleanest working bypass and has absorbed a large share of redirected flows, with further capacity additions 
planned for 2027. Iraq is leaning on the Kirkuk -Ceyhan pipeline and the broader Development Road corridor to 
Türkiye, while Oman’s Duqm port is the other candidate for a Gulf pipeline that bypasses the Strait altogether.  
Figure 10: Existing Hormuz bypasses and projects

Source: Allianz Research  
There is no easy fix for natural gas.  Unlike crude, LNG cannot be pipelined around Hormuz, which is why Qatari 
and Emirati LNG loadings have collapsed even as oil exports partially recovered via redirection. With normalization 
pushed out to Q1 2027, Qatar’s pattern of sending more empty tanke rs into the Gulf is one of the few signs of an 
eventual ramp-up, but there is no Petroline-equivalent for gas and the exposure is structural. 
On the demand side, the pressure to de -risk predates this crisis — it dates back to the war in Ukraine — and is 
now intensifying, though Russia/Ukraine plays no direct role in the current Hormuz shock beyond the refined -
products channel already noted.  China has spent the past decade building overland alternatives (the ESPO 
pipeline from Russia and the Kazakhstan -China link) specifically to cut seaborne Gulf and Malacca dependency, 
and its crude imports remain about 4mbpd below 2025 levels even at firm prices — though the recent uptick in 
Chinese buying is a refining -margin and re -export story more than fresh demand. India is diversifying toward US, 
West African and discounted Russian barrels via the Cape route while expanding strategic reserves, and Japan and 
South Korea are rebuilding SPRs drawn down earlier in the crisis. The India -Middle East-Europe Economic Corridor 
is likely to see fresh political momentum as a longer-term, non-maritime link between the Gulf and Europe. None of

### Page 10

10

this displaces Hormuz as the region’s central artery near -term, but it marks a durable shift toward supply -side 
redundancy and demand-side diversification that will outlast the current conflict.

## Document 181: A股三季度复盘：市场风格已悄然切换

Source PDF: all report pdf/A股三季度复盘：市场风格已悄然切换.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 7

### Page 1

⻛格切换的本质是资⾦ “ 集体缩短久期 ” 。受⽆⻛险利率抬升影响，市场不再为远期的 “ 想象
空间 ” 买单，转⽽追逐当期的 “ 确定性 ” 。上半年⼤涨的科技股重挫，资⾦⼤举涌⼊煤炭、银
⾏等年内能算清账的红利资产。接下来， 10 ⽉三季报将成为后市的核⼼定价锚。
摘要：
9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，三季度最后⼀个交易⽇。
沪指收 3842.19 点，涨 0.31% ；深证成指跌 0.11% ，创业板指跌 0.23% ，科创 50 跌 2.51% 。两市
成交 1.45 万亿元。
单看这⼀天，什么都没发⽣。放进整个季度，轮廓才清楚：创业板指三季度跌 27.8% ，科创 50
跌 30.7% ，双双创下各⾃历史上最⼤的单季跌幅。同期，煤炭涨 19.29% ，⽯油⽯化涨 15.32% ，
农林牧渔涨 15.10% ，银⾏涨 14.56% 。 31 个申万⼀级⾏业， 12 个上涨， 19 个下跌。
⼀句话来说，三季度的市场⻛格，本质是⼀次 “ 集体缩短久期 ” 。
图：同⼀季度，两种久期，两种命运。注： “ 久期 ” 的概念详⻅下⽂。

也就是说，同⼀份未来现⾦流，被按⼀个更⾼的利率重新算了⼀遍，仓位随之从 " 远 " 搬到
了 " 近 " 。
01  上半年的赢家，⼏乎就是三季度的输家
最刺眼的对照在电⼦板块，上半年涨 86% ，三季度跌 32.65% ，落差超过 118 个百分点。通信上
半年涨 74% ，三季度跌 28.72% 。建筑材料上半年涨 46% ，三季度跌 26.10% ，原因落在同⼀件事
的两⾯：它是半导体上游材料涨价的受益者，涨价预期停下来，估值就跟着停下来。
跌幅榜前列：电⼦ -32.65% 、通信 -28.72% 、建筑材料 -26.10% 、电⼒设备 -23.73% 、机械设
备 -21.11% 、基础化⼯ -13.56% 、国防军⼯ -12.59% 、有⾊⾦属 -10.46% 、计算机 -8.13% 。
涨幅榜前列：煤炭 19.29% 、⽯油⽯化 15.32% 、农林牧渔 15.10% 、银⾏ 14.56% 、医药⽣物
10.12% 、房地产 9.36% 、交通运输 6.44% 、美容护理 6.07% 、⻝品饮料 4.34% 。

有机构统计，申万 31 个⼀级⾏业上半年与三季度的涨跌幅相关系数为 -0.74 。轮动不会这么整
⻬，这更像是反转。

### Page 3

这三个⽉，基本⾯并没有掉头。中报⼝径下，中证⼈⼯智能指数成分股的归⺟净利润（ TTM ）
合计 1128.58 亿元，同⽐增⻓ 70.27% ；中证全指半导体指数成分股合计 942.92 亿元，同⽐增⻓
119.75% 。
业绩还在，甚⾄称得上强劲。可⻅，变化动因不在于业绩，⽽是市场愿意为这些业绩付多少
钱。
把时间拉⻓到前三季度，反差更⼤。
科创 50 累计上涨 13.82% ，是唯⼀收红的宽基指数；同期创业板指跌 2.12% ，深证成指跌
4.71% ，上证指数跌 3.19% ，北证 50 跌 27.83% 。同⼀个科技指数，年内还涨着 13.82% ，单个季
度却跌掉 30.70% 。创业板指的年内涨幅在 9 ⽉ 28 ⽇正式转负，⽽在 6 ⽉ 25 ⽇，它⼀度达到
36.49% 。三个⽉，从 +36.49% 到负数。
02  贴现率⼀动，先跌的是现⾦流最靠后的那批公司
三季度的问题，债券市场早就给过定义。
债券⾥有个指标叫久期，衡量价格对利率的敏感度：期限越⻓、现⾦流越靠后，久期就越⼤，

利率稍微⼀动，价格抖得越厉害。股票没有这个官⽅指标，但道理相通。⼀家公司的价值，是
它未来所有现⾦流折算回今天的总和；折现⽤的那个利率⼀旦抬升，被削得最狠的，是最后那
⼏年的权重。
2026 年 9 ⽉ 16 ⽇（当地时间），美联储把联邦基⾦利率⽬标区间上调 25 个基点，⾄ 3.75%—
4.00% 。这是 2023 年以来、也是两年降息之后的第⼀次转向。点阵图⾥， 18 名委员中有 16 ⼈预
计年底前⾄少还要加⼀次息，年底利率中值从 3.8% 上修⾄ 4.1% 。
图：折现率⼀抬，被削得最狠的是最后那⼏年
反应最先出现在债市。 9 ⽉， 10 年期美债收益率上⾏约 55 个基点⾄ 5.29% ，创 2007 年以来最⾼；
30 年期升⾄ 5.62% ，是 2002 年 6 ⽉以来的新⾼。
作为全球资产定价的锚，⽆⻛险利率站到 5% 以上，所有资产的 " 及格线 " 都被抬⾼了。

### Page 4

⼀家 2029 年才可能兑现利润的公司，和⼀家今年就能分红的公司，在 3% 的利率环境⾥，估值可
以很接近；但当⽆⻛险利率变成 5% ，前者必须拿出⾼得多的增⻓，才能证明⾃⼰值得被持有。
三季度，市场就是在做这道重新计算。
03  拥挤度到了极限，杠杆也到了极限
外部利率解释了 " 为什么是这个季度 " ，内部结构解释了 " 为什么跌得这么急 " 。
⼆季度，科创 50 单季涨 75.74% ，创业板指涨 36.35% 。 6 ⽉， TMT 板块成交额占全市场的⽐重触
及 43% 的历史极值。有机构测得，截⾄ 6 ⽉ 30 ⽇，创业板指相对中证红利指数的阶段性超额收益
达到 48.6% ；到 9 ⽉初，这部分超额基本回吐完毕。
杠杆资⾦同步到位。
6 ⽉ 23 ⽇， A 股两融余额⾸次突破 3 万亿元；上半年融资融券新开⼾ 96.06 万⼾，同⽐增加
56.96% 。
跌的过程并不平缓。 9 ⽉ 24 ⽇，沪指失守 3900 点，超 4300 只个股下跌；中秋休市后， 9 ⽉ 28 ⽇沪

指单⽇跌 1.66% ，创业板指跌 4.53% ，全市场超 4500 只个股下跌，深证成指与创业板指双双跌破
7 ⽉前低，主要指数多数失守半年线。
然后是三季度的退潮。
两融余额从⾼点回落，成交额把这个过程记录得最完整： A 股⽇均成交额从 6 ⽉的 3.13 万亿元，
降到 7 ⽉的 2.70 万亿元、 8 ⽉的 2.25 万亿元、 9 ⽉的 1.83 万亿元； 9 ⽉ 29 ⽇缩⾄ 1.42 万亿元，为近
14 个⽉新低，较今年 1 ⽉ 14 ⽇ 3.99 万亿元的阶段天量萎缩逾六成。
图：退潮有刻度：⽇均成交额逐⽉下台阶
想买的⼈， 6 ⽉⾥已经买完了。剩下的只有卖。

### Page 5

下跌也不均匀。 9 ⽉，创业板指跌 8.82% 、科创 50 跌 9.17% ，微盘股指数上涨 1.65% 。杀估值集
中在权重成⻓股⾝上。
6 ⽉底，全市场还有 199 只年内收益翻倍的主动权益基⾦；到三季度末，只剩下 2 只。同⼀时期，
偏股混合基⾦前三季度的平均收益率为 2.59% ，仍为正，但基⾦之间的⾸尾业绩差距被拉开到
163.56 个百分点。
同⼀场回调⾥，有⼈守住了，有⼈没有。
04  钱搬去了 " 今年就能算清 " 的地⽅
从⾼处撤出来的钱，没有离场，它换了个位置站着。
政策先给了⼀条线。
" 反内卷 " 从 2025 年底中央经济⼯作会议开始部署， 2026 年 3 ⽉写进政府⼯作报告，明确要⽤产能
调控、标准引领、价格执法、质量监管等⼿段整治内卷式竞争； 5 ⽉ 13 ⽇，市场监管总局启动为
期 8 个⽉的专项⾏动。
落到⾏业上：光伏发布 5 项强制性国家标准，其中 3 项能耗能效标准将于 2027 年 1 ⽉ 1 ⽇实施； 8
家多晶硅⻰头签署⾃律倡议，承诺销售价格不低于核算成本；汽⻋⾏业把 60 天账期从⾏业倡议

升级为国家层⾯的规范；⽔泥在东北、西南、华东等区域连续发布涨价函，单次上调 20 元⾄ 50
元 / 吨。
价格确实开始动了。 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇，秦皇岛港 Q5500 动⼒煤平仓价 983 元 / 吨，鄂尔多斯坑⼝含税价
760 ⾄ 830 元 / 吨； 8 ⽉我国进⼝动⼒煤 2763 万吨，同⽐下降 11% 。
政策的另⼀半落在需求端。 9 ⽉ 29 ⽇，财政部、央⾏、⾦融监管总局联合发布通知，⾃ 2026 年
10 ⽉ 1 ⽇起实施居⺠购房贷款贴息，政策实施期暂定 1 年，可享受贴息的贷款规模最⾼ 100 万元，
财政按年化 1 个百分点贴息，贴息期限最⻓ 5 年。房地产板块三季度上涨 9.36% ，涨的正是这条
贴息政策的确定性。
资⾦这条线，主⻆是险资。
险资已连续第五个季度加仓 A 股。到 2026 年 6 ⽉末， 42 家 A 股上市银⾏平均市净率 0.56 倍，全部
处于破净状态，按 2025 年分红测算有 13 家股息率超过 5% 。华泰证券的测算显⽰，保险⾏业上半
年已配置约 2.1 万亿元红利股，仍⽋配 1.9 万亿元。

政策给的是价格下限，险资给的是买盘下限。这两样东西有⼀个共同点：都能在今年的账上被
验证。
05  但 " 近处 " 也分真伪：农业涨 15% ，猪价跌到 10 元
涨幅榜第三的农林牧渔，最需要拆开看。
三季度涨 15.10% ，⽽它对应的现货价格，整个季度⾛得并不好。 9 ⽉⽣猪价格震荡下⾏， " ⾦
九 " 旺季落空， 9 ⽉底跌⾄约 10 元 / 公⽄的年内新低。供给端集中出栏、⼤猪压栏释放，需求端节
⽇备货偏弱，供需错配贯穿全⽉。
真正被买⼊的，是产能去化的预期。

### Page 6

⼆季度末，全国能繁⺟猪存栏调减⾄ 3780 万头，回到产能调控的绿⾊合理区间； 9 ⽉底前降⾄
3750 万头，被明确为硬指标，头部企业被要求带头压产、控体重。这属于政策承诺，还没有变
成当期利润。
它的逻辑能否成⽴，要等四季度猪价（能否）真的抬起来。
银⾏涨的逻辑⼜是另⼀套。
新会计准则下，⾼股息股票可以被指定为其他综合收益（ OCI ）类资产，市值波动不进利润表，
只有股息计⼊；低利率环境下，险资需要这样的现⾦流。这是资产负债表层⾯的再平衡。
图： “ 近处 ” 也分真伪：预期、事实与账⽬
⼀⾔以蔽之，在猪价上是预期，在煤价上是事实，在银⾏股息上是账⽬。
三者被⼀起买⼊，但只有后两者能在今年的报表上被验证。
06 10 ⽉，闸⻔先落在三季报上

新低。资⾦在同⼀个交易⽇⾥，把票投给了两种不同的时间。
接下来，估值的闸⻔将落到三季报上。
10 ⽉，上市公司将陆续披露三季报，这是四季度最重要的定价锚。

中报的强劲能否延续，此前 70.27% 的利润增速能否守住，直接决定科技⾏情是重建还是继续消
化。申万宏源策略的判断是，年内最好的⼀波进攻性机会，可能出现在 10 ⽉三季报消化估值之
后。
外部变量同样绕不开。

MACRO STRATEGY
Stronger Growth, Costlier Money
Chief Investment Office, Macro Strategy Team
The latest GDP report and annual revisions show a sturdier U.S. economy than previously
reported. According to the Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA), real GDP grew at a 2.2%
annualized pace in Q2 compared to 1.5% previously estimated, and Q1 was revised
from 2.1% to 2.5%. Both 2024 and 2025 were also revised slightly higher. Most of the

first half of the year revision was in categories related to the Artificial Intelligence (AI)
buildout, but real consumer spending has also been revised slightly higher, accelerating
from 0.7% annualized growth in Q1 to 3.8% in Q2 despite surging energy prices and
slow hiring.
Spending in July and August remained much stronger than labor market conditions alone
would suggest, also averaging 3.7% annualized growth. Upside revisions to net interest

income and personal saving suggest households have benefited from more income than
was previously apparent, with personal saving revised higher back to 2023. The new data,
according to the BEA, show the January to July 2026 savings rate averaged 4.8% versus
3.3% prerevision, strengthening consumer spending underpinnings despite moderate
hiring, high energy prices and rising borrowing costs.
The BEA update also included inflation methodological changes related to categories

inflated by the AI boom—such as portfolio management fees and computer software and
accessories. These improved the inflation picture to some extent—August “core” PCE
inflation was reduced by 0.3 ppts to 3% YoY, bringing it closer to “core” consumer price
index inflation, which was 2.5% YoY and 2% on a three-month annualized basis. While still
above the 2% Federal Reserve (Fed) target, “core” PCE now appears stable at 3% year over
year since January rather than accelerating. These methodological changes are expected

to dampen pass-through of AI-related price pressures into “core” inflation and effects of
stock-market swings on inflation.
They also produced an encouraging improvement in recent PCE inflation momentum.
Three-month annualized “core” PCE inflation slowed from 4.3% at the peak of tariff
effects in February to 2.1% in August. For durable goods, this rate was down from 5.8%
in February to 1.5% in August, and, for services ex-energy and healthcare, it slowed from

4.2% to 2.2%, for example. Inflation is volatile on a three-month change basis, so it
remains to be seen whether this moderation continues without further Fed tightening.
For now, the trends in the San Francisco Fed’s cyclical versus acyclical inflation measures
diverged again in August. As expected, given softening wage growth, prices most sensitive
to the labor market have eased further. At 3% YoY, this inflation category is down from
8% in early 2023 and 4.6% two years ago is back to prepandemic levels and is likely

to ease more (Exhibit 1A). In contrast, supply bottlenecks, high energy prices and other
industry-specific factors have kept acyclical inflation on an uptrend. As shown in Exhibit
1B, the relentless increase in freight costs could prolong upside pressure on this category.
The diesel market has tightened in recent months, boosting its price by 70% YoY,
consistent with Brent oil at around $200 per barrel and large reverberations across
a wide swath of goods and services prices. A government-policy-related decline in

available truck drivers is also raising transportation costs more than expected. So are
tight ocean shipping capacity and longer/more expensive voyages due to the U.S./Iran
War. Supply-chain stress also increased as companies rushed to front-run potential new
tariff increases.
On the positive side for the inflation outlook, unit labor costs (ULC) are up modestly
as robust productivity growth has absorbed much of the wage growth, limiting a major
source of domestic inflation. Upside productivity growth revisions are likely to further

reduce ULC, which could help shift focus from energy and AI-related inflation to
eventual disinflationary effects once oil and refined fuel prices start to ease. Sustained
productivity gains would support growth and margins while limiting inflation and the
need for much higher interest rates.
Investment Implications
We favor selective Equity exposure
to companies with durable demand
and pricing power, while remaining
cautious about businesses most

exposed to fuel, freight, and
financing costs. We would also
gradually increase long-duration
exposure.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Surging nominal growth has boosted corporate revenues (up 13% YoY in Q2, according to the
BEA) while contained unit labor costs helped raise margins and profits more than expected.
With labor costs declining as a share of revenues, investment-related tax incentives and
rock-bottom net interest expense, pre-tax nonfinancial corporate profits as measured in GDP
accounts rose 21% YoY in Q2. Total corporate profits after-tax were up 18.2% YoY, according to

the BEA.
Surging AI-related imports and investment are keeping the business and profits cycle on
an uptrend both here and abroad. According to BofA Global Research, the global economic
cycle backdrop improved further in September, as reflected in a broad-based increase in its
Global Wave1 indicator. Broadening U.S. manufacturing strength, fueled by ongoing AI-driven
investment, a healthier orders backlog and low inventories suggest that the expansion has

room to run—a constructive environment for earnings. Typically, a Global Wave upswing is
consistent with rising earnings revisions ratios and equity price uptrends. According to the
Global Wave, global earnings growth expectations remain exceptionally strong.
Still, absent a resolution of war-related energy and transportation disruptions, the same
industrial strength intensifies inflation pressures. A prolonged supply shock raises the risk of
restrictive interest rates and weakening economic growth. In this context, the surge in the

10-year Treasury yield above 5% is starting to reflect more than optimism about U.S. growth
and strengthening productivity outlook. Oil’s tightest recent correlation with 10-year Treasury
yields in 35 years suggests a common energy-price thread behind rising yields in developed
countries.
Bond market spillovers from normalizing Japan yields and capital repatriation are another
common thread. Higher yields on foreign sovereign bonds such as in Japan—which has

escaped stagnation and deflation by intensely pursuing investment-led growth—weaken the
relative appeal of Treasurys. That distinction matters for portfolios. A yield increase driven
mainly by durable productivity gains would come with a stronger outlook for future income
and earnings. A yield increase driven by cost shocks and greater uncertainty raises financing
costs without the same benefit.
In sum, the economy remains more resilient than expected, but the bond market is also

charging more for an uncertain inflation outlook given untethered government debt and
cooling global demand for Treasurys spurred by geopolitical tensions and Japan capital
repatriation. The case for much further tightening becomes stronger if wage growth
strengthens, productivity lags, and unit labor costs reaccelerate, causing cyclical inflation to
also pick up steam. The case becomes weaker if that measure stays contained while fuel and
freight pressures recede or rising Treasury yields reduce asset prices and economic growth.

MARKET VIEW
Yields, Yen and Iran: What Haunts the “House”
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
We recently highlighted the three-front challenge facing Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent:
engineering a stronger yen, containing the 10-year Treasury yield and securing a diplomatic
resolution to the conflict with Iran.

Bessent recently has delivered one of the year’s more memorable lines while discussing
Treasury policy and the yen: “I have asymmetric information. I am the house now.” 2 Yet several
challenges remain: the 10-year Treasury yield is near levels last seen in the early 2000s, the
yen remains structurally weaker, and a phased agreement to reopen the Strait of Hormuz
appears remote. The resulting strain on energy markets keeps inflation risks elevated.

Put differently, oil prices, Treasury yields and the dollar-yen exchange rate are all trending
against Bessent’s objectives. Below, we examine how each variable is haunting his “house.”
Haunting the “House” is the Yen. Since the U.S. and Japan mounted a record $98.7B
coordinated intervention in late July after the yen weakened to a 40-year low near 164 per
dollar, the currency has appreciated roughly 4% against the dollar. The move was further
supported on September 18 when the BoJ raised rates by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%,

their highest level since the mid-1990s (Exhibit 2A). However, the split 7–2 vote and Governor
Ueda’s limited forward guidance leave the path of further tightening uncertain. The key
question is whether additional BoJ hikes can produce sustained appreciation in the yen, or
merely reinforce the temporary relief provided by the summer intervention.
Bessent, meanwhile, has repeatedly signaled his preference for a stronger yen. Partly because
yen stability is also a U.S. financing concern. Persistent currency weakness could prompt

Japan to support its currency by selling foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys—which would
be an unwelcome prospect as Washington partially relies on foreign capital flows to fund its
large fiscal deficits. That potential “haunts” the Treasury Secretary.  At precisely the moment
Washington needs foreign capital to finance its enormous fiscal deficits, it would be inopportune
for one of America’s largest creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities.

Haunting the “House” are Yields. The surge in yields runs counter to a White House intent
on lowering borrowing costs. The benchmark 10-year yield has broken above the 5% threshold
and climbed to levels not seen since 2007. At around 5.30%, it stands more than a full
percentage point above where it began the year (Exhibit 2B). The level is striking but so is the
speed of the move: Stronger-than-expected economic data, including a survey showing U.S.

2 September 8, 2026.

While some key variables are
complicating the macro backdrop
(i.e., higher-for-longer yields/oil)
the combination of strong earnings
growth, robust consumer demand
(real final sales to private domestic
purchasers grew at 4.6% for Q2) and
ongoing capital investment suggests
that the fundamental backdrop for
U.S. Equities remains constructive.

5 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
business activity expanded at the fastest pace in more than five years, helped trigger an 18
bps surge in the 10-year yield—among its largest daily moves in decades. The 5-year yield also
topped 5% for the first time since 2007, while the 30-year yield reached 5.63%, its highest
level since 2002.
Compounding the move is inflation, running above the Fed’s 2% target for 67 months,

large fiscal deficits and the prospect of continued growth in Treasury issuance. Against this
backdrop, Treasury bought back $4.1 billion of long-dated debt, versus a maximum of $6
billion. That effort has done little to arrest the selloff. A subsequent auction priced nearly $4
billion of 7-year notes at 5.085%, the highest yield for that maturity in 33 years.
All in, the MOVE index, a key gauge of Treasury market volatility, jumped from 78 to 95 on

Wednesday September 23 and then to 104 the following day, its highest level since the early
days of the Iran war (Exhibit 3A).
Haunting the “House” is the Iran conflict. Early expectations that military pressure and
economic sanctions would quickly bring Iran to the negotiating table have yet to materialize.
Iranian officials have signaled that a full reopening of the Strait of Hormuz remains unlikely
while sanctions stay in place. Although talks between Washington and Tehran are continuing,

conflicting signals and lingering skepticism from both sides have kept tensions elevated.
As a result, oil prices have remained elevated in a range even as some Middle East supply
disruptions began to ease.
Startling is that Brent crude is up more than 60% this year, but refined product prices
have risen even faster. Ongoing disruptions to energy flows through the Strait of Hormuz,
combined with Ukrainian strikes on Russian refining infrastructure have tightened global

supplies of crude oil and refined fuels. Saudi Arabia has restored some drone-disrupted flows,
providing roughly 3.5 million barrels a day of relief (roughly 3% of global consumption).
The squeeze is mostly acute in diesel markets, where inventories remain constrained and
prices continue to climb (Exhibit 3B). U.S. diesel prices recently surpassed $6.50 per gallon,
the highest level on record, just as Russia has extended restrictions on diesel exports into

October. Against this backdrop, proposals to ban U.S. fuel exports risks worsening the
imbalance. According to S&P Global, an export ban could reduce refinery runs by nearly 2
million barrels per day and significantly cut gasoline production, potentially driving gasoline
and jet fuel prices even higher. As long as tensions persist, the oil impulse remains a key
impediment to structurally lower inflation.
Investment implications. Treasury Secretary Bessent increasingly sits at the intersection of

monetary policy, fiscal policy, foreign policy and national security. We remain constructive on
the U.S. economy and overweight U.S. Equities but are watching as these fronts challenge the
narrative. There is a great deal riding on the “house” versus the key macro variables of today.

6 of 8 October 5, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
When It Comes to Attracting Foreign Capital, the U.S. Remains Exceptional
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
There may be no better vote of confidence in a country than the willingness of
foreigners to put their money there. And by that measurement, the U.S. remains the
exception—both portfolio and FDI inflows are currently running at record highs based

on the latest data. This dynamic is one key reason why we remain constructive on U.S.
assets over the longer term.
Notwithstanding all the handwringing about America’s fiscal deficits, tariffs and rising
government debt, global investors sunk some $942B in net purchases of U.S. Equities in
the 12 months to July, the highest rolling 12-month total on record. In Q2, net purchase
of U.S. Equities soared to $426B, up 62% from the same period a year ago (Exhibit 4A).

By country, flows were down from China—not surprisingly—but strong from Taiwan and
South Korea, who have taken to recycling their massive AI-generated trade surplus back
into the U.S. In the end, strong portfolio inflows suggest that international investors
remain willing to increase their exposure to the world's largest capital market—that’s
bullish for U.S. assets.
Meanwhile, Exhibit 4B highlights the growing push among foreign multinationals to be

inside the U.S., with FDI inflows totaling nearly $240B in the first six months of this year.
That is a staggering 103% jump from a year ago and means FDI inflows are on track for
a record year, estimated at $480B for 2026.
There are multiple factors behind surging inflows: One, due to rising U.S. trade
protectionism, foreign multinationals increasingly want to be “inside” the U.S. economy,
not outside. Two, within the $32 trillion-plus economy with extraordinarily consumer

purchasing power, foreign firms prefer to be closer to their customers via investment
than just trade (exports). Three, America’s massive investment cycle in AI, data centers,
semiconductors, power generation, etc., has created numerous potential investment
opportunities for many foreign firms that don’t want to miss out on America’s surging
capex cycle. Four, and finally, abundant energy supplies, deep capital markets and a highly
developed technological ecosystem have also induced more FDI inflows.

In the end, double-barreled capital inflows speak to the absolute and relative
attractiveness of the U.S. economy and capital markets. Stay invested in America—the
rest of the world is.

is bullish for U.S. securities, both near-

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

ICE BofA MOVE Index is a market-implied measure of U.S. bond market volatility.
Consumer Price Index measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a market basket of consumer goods and services.
Baltic Dry Index is a daily shipping and trade index published by the Baltic Exchange in London that measures the cost of moving raw materials like iron ore, coal, and grain by sea.
Volatility Index (VIX) is a real-time market indicator that measures the stock market's expected volatility over the next 30 days.

Bank of America, Merrill, their affiliates and advisors do not provide legal, tax or accounting advice. Clients should consult their legal and/or tax advisors before making any financial decisions.
This material does not take into account a client’s particular investment objectives, financial situations, or needs and is not intended as a recommendation, offer, or solicitation for the purchase
or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and

of America, Merrill or any affiliate to sell or provide, or a specific invitation for a consumer to apply for, any particular retail financial product or service that may be available.
The Chief Investment Office (“CIO”) provides thought leadership on wealth management, investment strategy and global markets; portfolio management solutions; due diligence; and solutions
oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

## Document 253: India_ Growth, liquidity and policy response (2026-09-22)

GROWTH MOMENTUM TO SUSTAIN, 
EXTERNAL RISKS LINGER

India registered  strong growth at the start of 
FY27. Sustaining this performance will depend 
increasingly on how effectively it navigates a 
more uncertain global environment. Besides 
trade fragmentation, geopolitical tensions 
leading to higher energy prices, and shifts in 
international capital flows present key external 
risks.

India joined its Southeast Asian peers in 
posting an upside surprise on growth in 
1QFY27 (Apr -Jun26), up a firm 7.8%  yoy. few 
points of note:  
A) strong 1Q growth print reinforced the view 
that India’s domestic demand cycle was more 
resilient than initially assumed, with 
consumption, public capex and manufacturing 
providing a solid base despite external shocks.  
B) prevailing tailwinds by way of indirect tax 
relief in 4Q25, real rate buffer, and easier 
monetary conditions (earlier cuts), were also 
supportive;  
C) nominal GDP growth returned above 10% 
after two years on higher deflators, though 
some segments like manufacturing faced 
negative deflators as input prices rose at a 
faster pace than output due to commodity price 
increases and import intensity;  
D) Revisions to backdated numbers, to account 
for output PPI series, pushed up FY26 growth to 
7.8% (from 7.7% earlier). Two prior years were 
also revised.

www.dbs.com/insightsdirect/macro groupresc@dbs.com 
• High frequency indicators 
might keep  2QFY growth 
close to 7%. 
• Pace to moderate in 2HFY. 
• Retail inflation is likely to 
continue ris e and broaden  in 
scope, necessitating a tighter 
policy bias.  WPI likely to r ise 
by double-digits. 
• Special swap windows helped 
to stem bearish INR 
positioning. 
• Organic capital flows need to 
catch up.  
• Liquidity management is in 
focus, with a moderate 
correlation to inflation. 
• Twin-deficit risks are partly 
offset by stronger macro 
buffers.

High-frequency indicators

Into July -August, high frequency 
indicators are holding up, including GST 
e-way bills, indirect tax  revenues, 
electricity demand and growth in digital 
payments, while toll collections and 
petroleum consumption slipped . For 
demand, a contained extent of increase 
in retail pump prices shielded 
households from the surge in global 
crude prices, but other market linked 
fuel variants have climbed in recent 
months. On the employment front, job 
reductions were witnessed in the 
software sector, even as fresh 
opportunities were reported in global 
capability centres , electr onics/ 
semiconductors, healthcare, auto/ 
ancillary etc.  Wage growth in services 
has improved , manufacturing has been 
steady, while farm sector moderated, 
according t o consumption surveys by 
CMIE.

Industry indicators are a mixed bag, with 
PMIs maintaining their foothold in the 
expansionary terrain, while production 
and eight core index growth come off 
highs. Auto production continues to 
expand, though output and sales might 
moderate in the next quarter on base 
effects due to indirect tax cuts in the 
same time year ago. Forward-looking 
firm surveys have improved, after 
initially coming under pressure.

Sectors associated with rural demand  
might moderate amid weaker kharif 
sowing activity and lower reservoir 
levels impacting the rabi crop activity. 
Cumulative southwest monsoon is ~15% 
below the long-term average by tail end 
of the season. This takes the country on 
course to face its driest monsoon since 
2009 (which was 18% below LPA). 
Uneven geographical spread is notable, 
with south and east India registering 
nearly 25% shortfall.  While k harif 
sowing is on track with 99% of acreage 
completed, the winter crop might come 
under pressure as weather 
developments suggest El Niño  could 
extend into 2027.

### Page 3

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            3

With nominal GDP growth to track 11-12% 
this fiscal yea r, corporate earning s 
growth is expected to fare better than 
recent few years. 1QFY27 performance 
had already set off the year on a strong 
note, barring selected sectors like O&G 
which faced sequential marginal 
pressures from operating costs. Add to 
this, the centre’s capex gr owth is in firm 
double-digits, also backed by growth in 
core imports (non-oil non-gold).

On the external sector , despite better 
performance by exports, oil and 
electronics purchases have weighed on 
the goods math. The trade deficit is set 
to stay wide amidst elevated global 
crude cost, higher industrial fuels, 
cooking gas etc,  even as a wider mix of 
suppliers have addressed energy 
shortages. Notably, the net electronics 
deficit has widened considerably, just 
as exports have improved. Likewise, core 
imports (ex oil ex gold) have stayed firm, 
which partly feeds into higher exports as 
well as broader domestic demand. 
Monthly service trade surplus has been 
rangebound around $17 -18bn in recent 
months dwarfed by $25 -$30bn goods 
gap.

Maintaining growth forecasts

High frequency indicators put 2QFY 
growth close to 7%. We expect full year 
FY27 growth to average 7.3% yoy  from 
a revised 7.8% in FY26 , assuming 
moderation in the momentum in the 
second half of the year on tighter policy 
conditions, lagged impact of high energy 
prices and base effects from indirect tax 
cuts last year fade.  Assuming some 
spillover impact of exogenous 
uncertainties and tight financial 
conditions at home, we expect FY28 
growth to average 6.8-7.0%.

INFLATION OFF ITS BACK – 
THREE CATALYSTS

After two years of benign inflation, 
India’s price trend is off its back this 
year, mainly on the back of three

### Page 4

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            4

catalysts: Food, energy and early signs 
of demand pass through.

Aug inflation rose to a 20 -month high of 
4.8% yoy from 4.5% month before . As 
the chart highlights, number of sub -
segments growing by faster than the 
inflation target at 4% have continued to 
climb, pointing to a broadening out in the 
momentum.

Food inflation is gradually becoming 
more broad -based, shifting beyond 
vegetables and pulses to sugar, milk, 
protein groups, edible oils, amongst 
others. Sub-par weather has contributed 
to this upmove, besides idiosyncratic 
factors like swing in production  trends, 
diversion for fuel usage etc. After two 
years of normal monsoon, cumulative 
southwest rainfall this year is currently 
15% below the long -term average, 
alongside slower build -up in reservoir 
levels, which could impact upcoming rabi 
crops as well.  El-Nino patterns continue 
to strengthen, adding to seasonal swings 
and risk of impacting farm output.

Beyond food, g lobal oil prices have 
averaged ~$91pb in CY26, staying above 
$100pb for much of Seo26 . Retail fuel 
prices were adjusted in quick succession 
back in May26 and have been left 
unchanged since then , while CNG, 
aviation fuel, LPG etc have  continued to 
climb.

Transport inflation jumped to 4.6% yoy in 
Aug vs 0.1% in Jan26, besides utilities’ 
component inching up. Business price 
expectation surveys (conducted by IIM 
Ahmedabad) have, nonetheless, 
captured businesses facing a notable 
increase in fuel costs, especially 
industrial fuels, base metals and 
derivative product groups. Imported 
price pressures are most notabl e in the 
producer price/ wholesale price indices. 
These gauges have been hovering at 9 -
10% yoy in recent months.

### Page 5

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            5

Lastly, core inflation  (ex-food and fuel)  
rose to 1.5-month high in August, ma inly 
due to a sequential rebound in precious 
metals – gold and silver jewellery. 
Excluding precious metals, the reading 
was more moderate at 2.9%, pointing to 
some extent of economic slack. 
Nonetheless, other pockets like telecom, 
restaurants, business services, transport 
fares etc have been inching up, 
reflecting early signs of broadening in 
price pressures (on sequential basis), 
warranting attention. These are set 
against the backdrop of strong bank 
credit growth, flush INR liquidity and 
resilience in headline growth.

Our correlation analysis between 
liquidity (as % of NDTL) and core inflation 
– see chart, points to a positive but 
modest corelation at 0.4 between the 
two variables, with an assumed year -
worth lag on inflation. The inflation -
fighting mandate will also pus h the 
central bank to reduce the surplus 
liquidity in the domestic system.

A sh arp in global commodity prices is 
expected to fe ed into the wholesale 
price gauge , pushing up reading s to 
double-digits next quarter.

A gradual broadening of price 
pressures is likely to keep headline 
inflation above 5% in second half of the 
fiscal year , sharply narrowing the real 
rate buffer, underscoring the need for a 
tighter policy bias. Add to this, recent 
developments, including a sustained rise 
in crude prices, tightening global 
financial conditions, firm domestic 
growth and signs of broadening in core 
pressures, strengthen the case for a  
shallow 50bp hike in second half of FY27, 
making October’s meeting a live one.

TACKLING LIQUIDITY DELUGE, 
HIGHER YIELDS

Beating street estimates by a wide 
margin, the RBI’s special swap windows 
raised a cumulative $ 143bn into Sep , 
with the highest contribution (~92%) by

### Page 6

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            6

FCNR(B) deposits at $1 33bn, while rest 
were via offshore borrowing facilities. 
These flows pushed up the foreign 
reserves stock to a fresh high past 
$780bn, providing considerable 
firepower to defend the currency. This is 
already noticeable in the recent shift in 
the intervention bias. USDINR, which was 
earlier stubbornly steady in the face of 
strong FCNR inflows but  eased sharply 
due to strong intervention dollar sales 
and broader dollar swings, before being 
led higher due to external triggers.

Liquidity is a key watch factor. Given the 
swap arrangement, these inflows have 
added to already abundant INR liquidity 
backdrop, which was at a four year high, 
depressing overnight rates. The banking 
system surplus jumped past INR 10trn 
surpassing 2022 and Covid highs, with 
the durable balance widening sharply 
higher to INR 14trn, suppressing 
overnight call rates. This compares to 
average INR 1trn surplus in late-June.

While organic drivers like tax -related 
outflows, and seasonal currency leakage, 
in addition to CAD, portfolio outflows, 
and maturity of the forwards book will 
act as counter -balancing factors, yet 
concerted steps were required to drain 
the potential surge in liquidity.

We discussed the various seasonal, 
near-term and permanent measures  
that the central bank might consider (see 
FCNR bonanza necessitates deft 
management) to absorb this surfeit. 
Policymakers are being mindful of both 
the liquidity and signalling effects of 
the sterilisation tools at their disposal. 
The RBI utilised tranches of market 
operations like VRRR across tenors, 
after which three tranches of open 
market operations (bonds maturing FY29 
and FY32) worth a cumulative INR 1trn 
were conducted. These announcements 
fuelled a rise in yields, which were 
already inching up, tracking global 
counterparts. Sell/buy FX swaps for  
shorter rollovers were also being 
reportedly tapped.

When the dust settles, focus will also 
be on the bunched -up maturities that 
will fall due in 3Y and 5Y tenor of the 
deposits. A portion of the existing 
reserve stock could be earmarked 
against these liabilities, helping to 
mitigate concerns that deposit 
maturities or debt repayments could 
trigger a sharp increase in dollar demand 
and exert pressure on the FX market 
down the line. In the near-term, priorities 
will be to manage liquidity, gradually 
lower the sizeable forwards book, and 
support the domestic currency. Long -
end yields continue to be influenced by 
the hardening global yields.

BALANCE OF PAYMENTS MATH 
TO BE COMFORTABLE

The FY27 current account math is 
expected to display two key counter 
trends, Firstly, the wider goods trade 
imbalance will raise the FY27 current 
account deficit. Secondly, the 
counterbalancing effect of a sharp 
improvement in the capital flows math 
will keep the annual balance of 
payments math in surplus.

### Page 7

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            7

India recorded a current account deficit 
(CAD) of $10.5bn during April-July 2026, 
compared with $6.1bn in the 
corresponding period last year. The 
deterioration was driven almost entirely 
by a wider merchandise trade deficit, 
which rose to $117.8bn from $97.1bn a 
year ago. Part of this offset by higher  
income, transfers and capital account 
flow. Capital flows found support from 
net FDI, portfolio investments and 
banking capital, especially NRI deposits 
(jumped sharply to $36bn vs $4.6bn 
same period last year) , pushing up Apr -
Jul26 BOP to $12.7bn surplus, more than 
double of $4.8bn same time last year.

Oil prices are the main swing factor for 
the CAD this year , with the current run -
rate tracking our forecast at -1.1% of GDP 
for FY27. This suggests that the 
widening in the CAD is commodity -price 
related rather than driven by higher 
demand.

On the other hand, capital flows are 
being buttressed by the special swap 
windows introduced by the central bank, 
which raised a cumulative $ 143bn 
between June and August, through non -
resident deposits and external 
borrowings. This has helped to fil l the 
gap from subdued portfolio flows and 
net investment trends. We expect FY27 
current account deficit to track -1.1% of 
GDP in FY27, with the balance of 
payments to register upwards of $80bn 
surplus (vs -$23.6bn deficit last year).  
Into FY28, the dependency of capital 
flows on o rganic drivers like portfolio 
flows and FDI is set to increase, 
underscoring the need for higher 
vigilance.

FISCAL RUN-RATE

The FY27 fiscal run -rate points to a 
stronger revenue momentum, which 
has helped to keep the FYTD deficit in 
check, even as expenditure was 
frontloaded owing to subsidy payments 
and capex disbursements. April-July 
2026 deficit stands at a third of the full-
year balance.

### Page 8

India: Growth, liquidity and policy response September 22, 2026 
                                                                                                                                            8

Both tax and non -tax receipts fared 
better than the comparable period year 
ago, helped by stronger corporate tax 
collections, STT transaction revenues, 
and RBI dividend, amongst others.

Setting 2QFY for a strong start, net 
direct tax collections r ose 13% yoy by 
mid-Sep26, driven by net corporate tax 
collections up 19.5%. Net non -corporate 
tax collections were up 6%, besides STT 
collections up a sharp 53% yoy. Add to 
this, divestments have also fared better 
than expected this year, with ~78% 
raised by August, accompanied by 
dividends & profits (at 83% of budget by 
Jul26).

Under expenditure, about 33% of the full 
year allocation was allocated by July 
2026. The subsidy outgo rose sharply, up 
35% yoy, led by higher food at 23% and 
fertiliser at 46% (already accounting for 
nearly two-third of the full year budget)  
led by surging global DAP and urea 
prices. With global prices remaining high, 
the actual outgo might be higher than 
budgeted INR 1.7trn , likely towards INR 
2.5trn. Capital spending also remained 
apace, up 30% yoy in first five months of 
the year.

Overall, strong growth in tax and non-tax 
receipts allowed the centre to sustain 
higher spending while containing the 
extent of fiscal slippage until July 2026 
(FYTD). A strong nominal GDP rate 
(helped by higher inflation) will also 
provide a favourable denominator effect.

IN SUM

The economy exhibited resilience in the 
face of a tough external environment in 
the first quarter, with part of that 
stability likely to extend to 2QFY27 as 
well. Tighter monetary conditions, El 
Nino impact and base effects from 
passage of post -indirect tax jump will 
moderate headline growth in 2HFY. 
Nonetheless for the year, we expected 
growth to stay north of 7%, factoring in a 
strong start.

## Document 281: OpenAI宣布GPT-6提速50%，额度却被锤只有Claude的1_5

Source PDF: all report pdf/OpenAI宣布GPT-6提速50%，额度却被锤只有Claude的1_5.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 9
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

为什么要收紧订阅？ SemiAnalysis 算了⼀笔账。订阅是⾼度补贴的产品，以  Anthropic 为
例，据估算，订阅只占总收⼊的约  10% ，却可能吃掉  40% 以上的推理算⼒，让每兆⽡的
混合收⼊减少约  3600 万美元。对订阅收⼊占⽐更⾼的  OpenAI 来说，这个问题只会更突
出。
摘要：
昨天， Tibo 承诺「连续  28 天发布或重置」。刚刚，⾸个更新来了。
Tibo 宣布， OpenAI 已优化默认速度：订阅⽤⼾在所有产品中使⽤  GPT-6 Astra 和  GPT-6.1
Sol ，速度提升约  50% 。⽤⼾⽆需做任何操作，两⼩时内就能感受到变化。
同时， OpenAI 官⽅还宣布了⽂本⽔印功能：
⽽就在⼏乎同⼀时间， SemiAnalysis 发布了⼀份订阅实测报告，标题毫不客⽓：Anthropic 订
阅提供的价值是  OpenAI 的  5 倍以上。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⽣活家 搜索

### Page 2

OpenAI 可能也没想到，⾃⼰刚兑现承诺就被  SemiAnalysis 这家著名第三⽅机构正⾯打脸。不
仅如此，就连  OpenAI 这次更新本⾝，也被很多⽹友吐槽⼒度不够，不如⼀次重置。
昨天我们写到，开发者⽇之后， OpenAI 正⾯临⼀场⽤⼾信任危机，重度⽤⼾的不满集中在两件
事上：模型慢，额度少。第  1 天回应了前者， SemiAnalysis 的报告则把后者摆上了台⾯。
第  1 天：先解决「慢」
Tibo 随后在回复中补充了数字：输出速度从  30 TPS 提升到  50 TPS ，并搭配了他所说的⽬前优
化程度最⾼的  tokenizer 。他还特别强调，这两个模型完成任务所需的  token 数量本⾝就很少。
这次提速不只覆盖  OpenAI ⾃家产品，也包括所有通过「 Sign in with ChatGPT 」接⼊的合作伙
伴， Tibo 点名了  OpenCode 、 Pi 、 Amp 、 Devin 等。换句话说，只要是⽤  ChatGPT 订阅跑这
两个模型，⽆论在哪⾥调⽤，都会变快。

但有开发者在评论区表⽰，即便如此， ChatGPT 模型依然⽐  Claude 慢。

### Page 3

还有⼀些开发者则在评论区表⽰了质疑，并有⼀些实测结果晒出，⽐如  @BrenBuilds 晒出的实
测数据显⽰， GPT-6.1 的速度不过是回到了该模型发布当前的速度。
同样花钱，差了  5 倍
但速度不等于额度。第  1 天的更新没有提到任何使⽤限额的变化，⽽这正是  SemiAnalysis 报告
的主⻆。
这份报告对  Anthropic 、 OpenAI 、 Meta 、 SpaceXAI 、 MiniMax 、⽉之暗⾯（ Moonshot ）、智谱
（ Z.ai ）、 Cursor 和  Cognition 的订阅⽅案逐⼀做了极限测试。
订阅⽅案不会公开细粒度的价格，⽤⼾只能看到⼀个  0 到  100% 的⽤量进度条，通常分为  5 ⼩
时和  7 天两个窗⼝。

### Page 4

SemiAnalysis 的做法是把输⼊、缓存写⼊、缓存读取、输出四类  token 逐⼀隔离，⽤专⻔设计
的  prompt 反复调⽤，记录进度条每跳⼀格消耗了多少  token ，直到误差收敛到  ±5% 以内。最
后再按  API 价格折算，得到每份订阅的「 API 等价价值」，也就是这份额度如果按  API 计费值多
少钱。
SemiAnalysis 发现，同⼀家⼚商的三个相同订阅⾥，有⼀个账号的额度⽐另外两个低约  20% 。
与⼚商沟通后才确认，这个账号被分进了⼀个规模极⼩的额度  A/B 测试。这也说明，⼚商可以
随时、静默地调整订阅额度，普通⽤⼾⼏乎⽆从察觉。
先看旗舰档。 GPT-6 Astra 和  Claude Fable 5.1 的额度其实相当接近，区别在于  Fable 在
Claude 订阅中最多只能占⽤总额度的⼀半。在  200 美元档位上，⽤掉价值  2485 美元的  Fable
5.1 之后， Claude 订阅还剩⼀半额度可以留给其他模型；同档  OpenAI 订阅在消耗完价值  2897

美元的  Astra 后，就彻底⻅底了。
真正拉开差距的是中档模型，也就是两家都定位为⼤多数⽤⼾「⽇常主⼒」的  Claude Opus 5.5
和  GPT-6.1 Sol 。在这⼀层级， Anthropic 订阅的  API 等价价值约为  OpenAI 的  5 倍，并且在各

### Page 5

个价位档全⾯领先。
有⼈会说，这样⽐对  OpenAI 不公平： 6.1 Sol 的  API 单价本就⽐  Opus 5.5 便宜得多，按  API
价格折算⾃然吃亏。 SemiAnalysis 预判了这个质疑，即便改为直接⽐较  token 数量，差距依然
很⼤。
OpenAI 能拿出来的唯⼀反驳，是它的  Pro 套餐没有  5 ⼩时限制，⽤⼾更容易把⽉度额度真正
⽤满。但  SemiAnalysis 认为，这不⾜以抵消  Opus 5.5 在  Claude 订阅上约  4 倍的价值优势。
报告判断：独⽴开发者过去常常称赞  OpenAI 的订阅额度⽐  Anthropic 慷慨，这个说法如今已经
不成⽴了。
额度砍半， 500 美元档只多  21%
差距之所以这么⼤，很⼤程度上来⾃  OpenAI 上周的那次调整，参阅《 OpenAI 将重新开放  200
美元套餐，但  Tibo 被骂惨了》。
SemiAnalysis 的追踪数据证实了  Tibo 此前宣布的变化： OpenAI 200 美元套餐的  API 等价价值

被砍掉⼀半。具体来说，每个模型档位可⽤的  token 数量都减半；⼜因为  GPT-6.1 Sol 同时下
调了缓存输⼊价格， Sol 级模型的  API 等价价值跌幅超过  50% 。降额前购买的  200 美元套餐可
以保留旧额度到  10 ⽉  29 ⽇，新购⽤⼾则⽴即适⽤新额度。

### Page 6

同期推出的  500 美元新档位，Astra 额度只⽐旧版  200 美元套餐多  21% ，Sol 级模型的  API 等
价价值甚⾄不升反降。这⼀档真正的卖点是  300 TPS 的  Ultrafast 模式， SemiAnalysis 表⽰其
额度仍在测试中。
在此之前， 200 美元档是  OpenAI 补贴⼒度最⼤的套餐。按每美元价值计算， Pro 100 在  Astra
上约为  Plus 的  2 倍， Pro 200 ⼜在  Pro 100 的基础上再翻⼀倍。调整之后， Pro 100 、 200 和
500 三档每美元能换到的  token 完全⼀致，「买⼤档更划算」的逻辑不复存在。
很多⽤⼾的抱怨「付的钱没少，⽤量变少了」，现在有了第三⽅数据作注脚。当然，这个第三⽅
数据本⾝也受到了⼀些质疑：

### Page 7

两种降补贴思路
为什么要收紧订阅？ SemiAnalysis 算了⼀笔账。订阅是⾼度补贴的产品，以  Anthropic 为例，
据估算，订阅只占总收⼊的约  10% ，却可能吃掉  40% 以上的推理算⼒，让每兆⽡的混合收⼊减
少约  3600 万美元。
对订阅收⼊占⽐更⾼的  OpenAI 来说，这个问题只会更突出。
两家都想降低补贴，路径却截然不同。
Anthropic ⾛的是渐进路线：越⾼端的模型，订阅⾥给的  API 等价价值越低。 Sonnet 5.5 与
Opus 5.5 之间差距不⼤，到了  Fable 5.1 则明显收缩。 SemiAnalysis 测算，在  100% 使⽤率、
API ⽑利率  92% 的假设下，⽤满  Opus 5.5 对应的订阅⽑利率是  -369% ，⽤满  Fable 5.1 约为
1% ；如果按更现实的  20% 平均使⽤率计算，两者分别为  6% 和  80% 。换句话说，如果所有⼈
只⽤  Fable ， Anthropic 的订阅业务很可能已经拥有软件公司级别的利润率。

### Page 8

OpenAI 选的则是被  SemiAnalysis 称为「核选项（ nuclear option ）」的做法：直接把所有模型
的额度⼀⼑切，降到  Fable 级别。
按理说，这种做法应该招致强烈反弹。但  SemiAnalysis 认为， OpenAI 在很⼤程度上躲过了这
⼀劫，可能的原因是开发者⽇的⼤量正⾯宣传冲淡了前⼀天的降额消息，加上⽼⽤⼾还能多保
留⼀个⽉旧额度。
只是从这⼏天社交媒体上的情绪来看，这次「躲过」也许只是推迟。
剩下的  27 天
第  1 天的提速是实打实的改进。 Tibo 也给出了  OpenAI ⾃⼰的辩护逻辑：模型完成任务需要的
token 更少，单⽐  token 数量或  API 等价价值，并不能完全反映实际产出。这个论点有⼀定道
理， SemiAnalysis ⾃⼰也承认，当某个模型按  API 价格看特别贵或特别便宜时， API 等价价值
会失真。
但「每个任务能省多少  token 」⽬前还没有第三⽅的量化数据，⽽额度减半已经是被测出来的事
实。

还有⼀个时间点需要留意：降额前购买的  200 美元套餐，旧额度将在  10 ⽉  29 ⽇到期，正好落
在这  28 天的窗⼝期内。到那时，此前被「保护」起来的⼀批重度⽤⼾，也会直接感受到额度的
变化。

### Page 9

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。
